The limit of \(\frac{\sin x}{x}\) as \(x\) approaches 0 can be determined using both numerical evaluation and analytical methods.

**Numerical Approach:**
- As \(x\) approaches 0, the value of \(\frac{\sin x}{x}\) was calculated for small values such as 0.1, 0.01, and 0.001.
- The results consistently approached 1, suggesting that the limit is likely 1.

**Analytical Approach (Squeeze Theorem):**
- For \(0 < x < \frac{\pi}{2}\), it holds that \(\cos x \leq \frac{\sin x}{x} \leq 1\).
- As \(x\) approaches 0, both \(\cos x\) and the upper bound approach 1.
- By the Squeeze Theorem, \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1\).

**L’Hospital’s Rule:**
- Both the numerator (\(\sin x\)) and denominator (\(x\)) approach 0 as \(x\) approaches 0, resulting in an indeterminate form \(0/0\).
- Applying L’Hospital’s Rule:
  \[
  \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos x}{1} = \cos(0) = 1
  \]

**Conclusion:**
All methods consistently show that the limit is 1.

Next, I differentiate both the numerator and the denominator with respect to \(x\).

The derivative of the numerator \(\ln(1+x)\) is \(\frac{1}{1+x}\), and the derivative of the denominator \(x\) is 1.

Applying L'Hospital's Rule, the limit becomes:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{1+x}
\]

Finally, substituting \(x = 0\) into the simplified expression gives:

\[
\frac{1}{1+0} = 1
\]
</think>

To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Indeterminate Form:**
   - When \(x\) approaches 0, both the numerator \(\ln(1 + x)\) and the denominator \(x\) approach 0.
   - This results in an indeterminate form of type \(\frac{0}{0}\).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Since we have an indeterminate form \(\frac{0}{0}\), we can use L'Hospital's Rule, which states that:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
     \]
     provided the limit on the right exists.

   - Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \(x\):
     - The derivative of the numerator \(\ln(1 + x)\) is:
       \[
       f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \ln(1 + x) = \frac{1}{1 + x}
       \]
     - The derivative of the denominator \(x\) is:
       \[
       g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} x = 1
       \]

   - Therefore, applying L'Hospital's Rule gives:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\frac{1}{1 + x}}{1}
     \]

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   - Simplify the expression after differentiation:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1}{1 + x}
     \]
   
4. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   - Now, substitute \(x = 0\) into the simplified expression:
     \[
     \frac{1}{1 + 0} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

The limit of (1 + 1/x)^x as x approaches infinity is a well-known result in calculus. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Expression:** We are given the expression \((1 + \frac{1}{x})^x\) and asked to find its limit as \(x\) approaches infinity.

2. **Recognize the Fundamental Limit:** The expression resembles a standard limit in calculus, which is:
   \[
   \lim_{n \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{n}\right)^n = e
   \]
   Here, \(e\) is Euler's number approximately equal to 2.71828.

3. **Apply the Known Limit:** Directly applying this standard limit to our expression gives:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x = e
   \]

4. **Verification Through Logarithms (Optional):** To confirm, take the natural logarithm of the expression and simplify:
   - Let \(y = \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x\).
   - Take the natural log: \(\ln(y) = x \cdot \ln\left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)\).
   - Expand using Taylor series for small \(t = \frac{1}{x}\): \(\ln(1 + t) \approx t - \frac{t^2}{2} + \cdots\).
   - Substitute back: \(\ln(y) \approx x\left(\frac{1}{x} - \frac{1}{2x^2} + \cdots\right)\), simplifying to \(1 - \frac{1}{2x} + \cdots\).
   - As \(x \to \infty\), the additional terms vanish, so \(\ln(y) \to 1\) and thus \(y \to e^1 = e\).

5. **Conclusion:** Both direct recognition of the standard limit and verification through logarithmic expansion confirm that the limit is indeed \(e\).

The relation between \( x \) and \( h \) is given by \( x = h + \frac{h^2}{2} \). Thus, the final answer is \boxed{x = h + \frac{1}{2}h^2}.
</think>

Given the equation:

\[
\sqrt{\frac{1+x}{1-x}} = \frac{1}{1-h}
\]

we need to find the relationship between \( x \) and \( h \).

First, we square both sides to eliminate the square root:

\[
\frac{1+x}{1-x} = \left(\frac{1}{1-h}\right)^2
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
\frac{1+x}{1-x} = \frac{1}{(1-h)^2}
\]

Rewriting this, we get:

\[
1 + x = \frac{1 - x}{(1 - h)^2}
\]

Multiplying both sides by \( (1 - h)^2 \):

\[
(1 + x)(1 - h)^2 = 1 - x
\]

Expanding the left side:

\[
(1 + x)(1 - 2h + h^2) = 1 - x
\]

Distributing terms:

\[
1 - 2h + h^2 + x - 2xh + xh^2 = 1 - x
\]

Subtracting 1 from both sides and simplifying:

\[
-2h + h^2 + x - 2xh + xh^2 = -x
\]

Rearranging terms:

\[
x(1 - 2h + h^2) + (h^2 - 2h) = -x
\]

Combining like terms and solving for \( x \):

\[
x(1 - 2h + h^2 + 1) = 2h - h^2
\]

Simplifying further:

\[
x(2 - 2h + h^2) = 2h - h^2
\]

Dividing both sides by \( 2 - 2h + h^2 \):

\[
x = \frac{2h - h^2}{2 - 2h + h^2}
\]

For small values of \( h \), we approximate the denominator using a series expansion:

\[
x \approx \frac{2h}{2} = h
\]

Considering higher-order terms, we find that:

\[
x \approx h + \frac{h^2}{2}
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{x = h + \frac{1}{2}h^2}
\]

In this problem, our expression inside the parentheses is \( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \), and it's raised to the power of \( x \). Comparing this with the standard formula, we can see that the "constant" in the numerator is 2.

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)^x = e^2
\]

Therefore, the value of the limit is \( e^2 \).

1. **Differentiate each term separately**:
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) is \( 6x \).
   - The derivative of \( -5x \) is \( -5 \).
   - The derivative of the constant \( 4 \) is \( 0 \).

2. **Combine the derivatives**:
   \[

To find the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \), we can use basic differentiation rules.

**Step 1:** Rewrite the function.
\[
\frac{1}{x} = x^{-1}
\]

**Step 2:** Apply the power rule for derivatives, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = n x^{n-1} \).

**Step 3:** Differentiate \( x^{-1} \).
\[
\frac{d}{dx} x^{-1} = -1 \cdot x^{-2} = -\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

Thus, the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
-\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

The derivative of \( e^{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is calculated using the fundamental rule of calculus for exponential functions. Specifically, the derivative of \( e^{u(x)} \) is \( e^{u(x)} \cdot u'(x) \). Since \( u(x) = x \), its derivative \( u'(x) \) is 1. Therefore, multiplying these together gives \( e^{x} \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Function**: The function given is \( e^{x} \).
2. **Apply the Exponential Derivative Rule**: The rule states that the derivative of \( e^{u(x)} \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^{u(x)} \cdot u'(x) \).
3. **Determine \( u(x) \)**: Here, \( u(x) = x \), so \( u'(x) = 1 \).
4. **Compute the Derivative**: Multiply \( e^{x} \) by \( 1 \) to get \( e^{x} \).

**Answer:** The derivative of \( e^{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^{x} \).

To find the derivative of the polynomial \( x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \), we will apply the power rule and basic differentiation rules to each term individually. Here's a step-by-step solution:

### Step 1: Differentiate Each Term

1. **First Term (\( x^3 \))**:
   - Apply the power rule: \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = nx^{n-1} \)
   - Here, \( n = 3 \), so:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} x^3 = 3x^{2}
     \]

2. **Second Term (\( 2x^2 \))**:
   - Apply the constant multiple rule: \( \frac{d}{dx} [c \cdot f(x)] = c \cdot f'(x) \)
   - Differentiate \( x^2 \) using the power rule:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} 2x^2 = 2 \cdot 2x^{1} = 4x
     \]

3. **Third Term (\( x \))**:
   - Apply the power rule for \( n = 1 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} x = 1
     \]

4. **Fourth Term (\( 1 \))**:
   - The derivative of a constant is zero:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} 1 = 0
     \]

### Step 2: Combine the Derivatives

Now, combine the derivatives of each term:

\[
\begin{align*}
\frac{d}{dx}(x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1) &= \frac{d}{dx} x^3 + \frac{d}{dx} (2x^2) + \frac{d}{dx} x + \frac{d}{dx} 1 \\
&= 3x^{2} + 4x + 1 + 0 \\
&= 3x^{2} + 4x + 1
\end{align*}
\]

### Final Answer

The derivative of the function is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "3x² + 4x + 1"}
```

The derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using the chain rule.

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we can use the quotient rule. 

The quotient rule states that if we have a function \( \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:
\[
\left( \frac{u}{v} \right)' = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
\]

For our function, let \( u(x) = e^x \) and \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \).

First, we find the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \):
- The derivative of \( u(x) = e^x \) is \( u'(x) = e^x \).
- The derivative of \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \) is \( v'(x) = e^x \).

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{e^x (1 + e^x) - e^x \cdot e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:
\[
e^x (1 + e^x) - e^{2x} = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x
\]

Thus, the derivative is:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Therefore, the derivative of the function is \boxed{\dfrac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}}.

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using implicit differentiation.

Let \(y = \tan^{-1} x\), so \(\tan y = x\).

Differentiating both sides with respect to \(x\):
\[
\sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Solving for \(\frac{dy}{dx}\):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
\]

Using the identity \(\sec^2(y) = 1 + \tan^2(y)\) and substituting \(\tan y = x\):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
\]

Thus, the derivative is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{1 + x^{2}}}
\]

The derivative of \(\sec^2 x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using the chain rule.

1. Let \(u = \sec x\), so that \(f(x) = u^2\).
2. The derivative of \(f(x)\) with respect to \(x\) is:
   \[
   f'(x) = 2u \cdot \frac{du}{dx}
   \]
3. Since \(\frac{du}{dx} = \sec x \tan x\), substituting back gives:
   \[
   f'(x) = 2 \sec x \cdot (\sec x \tan x) = 2 \sec^2 x \tan x
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': '2 sec²(x) tan(x)'}

To find the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Highest Degree Terms:**  
   Both the numerator and denominator are polynomials of degree 2.

2. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by \( x^2 \):**
   \[
   \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{\frac{3x^2}{x^2} + \frac{2x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{\frac{5x^2}{x^2} + \frac{4x}{x^2} + \frac{2}{x^2}}
   \]

3. **Simplify:**
   \[
   = \frac{3 + \frac{2}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{5 + \frac{4}{x} + \frac{2}{x^2}}
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Limit as \( x \to \infty \):**
   - As \( x \) approaches infinity, terms with \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach 0.
   
5. **Final Simplification:**
   \[
   = \frac{3 + 0 + 0}{5 + 0 + 0} = \frac{3}{5}
   \]

**Answer:**  
\boxed{\dfrac{3}{5}}

The limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side can be evaluated by considering the natural logarithm and applying L'Hospital's Rule.

**Solution:**

Let \( y = x^x \).

Take the natural logarithm:
\[
\ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

Find the limit of \( \ln(y) \) as \( x \to 0^+ \):
\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} \ln(y) = \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

Rewrite this as:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x}
\]

This is an indeterminate form \( \frac{-\infty}{+\infty} \), so apply L'Hospital's Rule:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}\ln(x)}{\frac{d}{dx}(1/x)} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{-\frac{1}{x^2}} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} (-x) = 0
\]

Exponentiate to find \( y \):
\[
y = e^{0} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': '1'}

The given limit represents the derivative of \( f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \) at \( x = 1 \).

To find this derivative, we use the chain rule:
- Let \( u = 1 + x^3 \), so \( \frac{du}{dx} = 3x^2 \).
- Then, \( f(u) = u^{1/3} \), so \( \frac{df}{du} = \frac{1}{3}u^{-2/3} \).

Combining these:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{df}{dx} = \frac{df}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = \frac{1}{3} (1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}
\]

Evaluating at \( x = 1 \):
\[
f'(1) = \frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}}
\]

Thus, the limit is \( \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} \).


The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using the rule that the derivative of \( a^x \) is \( \ln(a) \cdot a^x \). For \( a = e \), since \( \ln(e) = 1 \), the derivative simplifies directly to \( e^x \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Function:** The function given is \( f(x) = e^x \).
2. **Apply the Exponential Derivative Rule:** The general rule for differentiating an exponential function with base \( a \) is:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (a^x) = (\ln a) \cdot a^x
   \]
3. **Substitute \( a = e \):** Since the base here is \( e \), we substitute into the rule:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = (\ln e) \cdot e^x
   \]
4. **Simplify Using Natural Logarithm:** Knowing that \( \ln e = 1 \), this simplifies to:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = 1 \cdot e^x = e^x
   \]

**Answer:**
The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^x \).

   - Here, \( n = 3 \), so the derivative is \( 3x^{2} \).

   - Again, applying the power rule.
   - The derivative of \( x^2 \) is \( 2x \).
   - Multiplying by the coefficient 2, we get \( 4x \).

3. **Differentiate \( x \):**
   - The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.

4. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   - The derivative of a constant is 0.

5. **Combine all derivatives:**
   - Adding up all the individual derivatives:
     \[
     f'(x) = 3x^{2} + 4x + 1
     \]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we use the quotient rule. 

Let:
- \( g(x) = \ln x \) so \( g'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
- \( h(x) = x^2 \) so \( h'(x) = 2x \)

\[

\[

\]

   Using the chain rule,  
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = 2x e^{x^2}.

   Differentiate \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \) using the product rule:

   \[

Wait, what's the chain rule again? It says that if you have a composite function, say f(g(x)), then the derivative is f’(g(x)) * g’(x). So in this case, my outer function f(u) = u² and the inner function g(x) = sec(x).

To find the approximate difference in the sine function for a small change \( h = 0.0001 \) around \( x = \frac{\pi}{4} \):

1. The given angle is \( x = \frac{\pi}{4} \).
2. The change in \( x \) is \( h = 0.0001 \).
3. Use the linear approximation for small changes: 
   \[
   \Delta y \approx \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) \cdot h
   \]
4. Calculate \( \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) \):
   \[
   \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}
   \]
5. Substitute into the approximation:
   \[
   \Delta y \approx \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \cdot 0.0001
   \]
6. Compute the value:
   \[
   \Delta y \approx 0.7071 \times 0.0001 = 0.00007071
   \]

Thus, the approximate difference is \(\boxed{0.000071}\).

The function \(2x^2 - 5x + 1\) is a polynomial, and polynomials are continuous everywhere. Therefore, to find the limit as \(x\) approaches 3, we can simply substitute \(x = 3\) into the function.

Calculating each term:
- \(2(3)^2 = 2*9 = 18\)
- \(-5(3) = -15\)
- The constant term is +1

Adding them together: 18 - 15 + 1 = 4

So, the limit as \(x\) approaches 3 of \(2x^2 - 5x + 1\) is 4.

**Step-by-step explanation:**

1. Identify that the function is a polynomial.
2. Recall that polynomials are continuous everywhere.
3. Substitute \(x = 3\) into each term:
   - \(2(3)^2 = 18\)
   - \(-5(3) = -15\)
   - The constant term is +1
4. Add the results: 18 - 15 + 1 = 4

Both sin(x) and x are differentiable around x = 0, except possibly at x = 0 itself, but they're both smooth there.

Now evaluating this at x = 0 gives cos(0), which is 1. Therefore, the limit should be 1.

The limit evaluates as follows:

We need to find:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{x}
\]

As \( x \) approaches 0, both the numerator (\(\sin x\)) and denominator (\(x\)) approach 0, resulting in an indeterminate form of \(\frac{0}{0}\). Applying L’Hospital’s Rule:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{x} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\cos x}{1} = \cos(0) = 1
\]

The given expression is \(\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}\).

First, factor the numerator:
\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

Substitute this back into the expression:
\[
\frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
\]

Cancel out the common factor of \(x - 1\) (since \(x \neq 1\)):
\[
x^2 + x + 1
\]

Now, take the limit as \(x\) approaches 1:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
\]

**Answer:**  
The limit is \(\boxed{3}\).

The limit of \(\frac{e^x}{x^2}\) as \(x\) approaches infinity is determined by recognizing that the exponential function \(e^x\) grows much faster than any polynomial, including \(x^2\). Applying L'Hospital's Rule twice confirms this:

1. First derivative: \(\frac{d}{dx} e^x = e^x\) and \(\frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x\), resulting in \(\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{e^x}{2x}\).
2. Second derivative: \(\frac{d}{dx} e^x = e^x\) and \(\frac{d}{dx} 2x = 2\), leading to \(\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{e^x}{2}\).

Since \(e^x\) grows without bound as \(x\) approaches infinity, the limit is also unbounded.

This is the formal definition of a limit approaching negative infinity.

To find the limit of √(x² + 2x) - x as x approaches infinity, I can start by simplifying the expression inside the square root. For very large values of x, the term x² dominates over 2x. Therefore, √(x² + 2x) is approximately equal to √(x²) which simplifies to x.

Substituting this approximation into the original expression gives:

√(x² + 2x) - x ≈ x - x = 0

However, since there's a linear term in the square root, we need to refine our approximation. Let's factor out x from inside the square root:

√(x² + 2x) = √[x²(1 + (2/x))] = |x|√(1 + (2/x))

Since x is approaching infinity and thus positive, this simplifies to x√(1 + (2/x)).

Now, we can use the binomial approximation for square roots:

√(1 + ε) ≈ 1 + (ε/2) - (ε²/8) + ... where ε is small.

Applying this to our expression with ε = 2/x:

x√(1 + (2/x)) ≈ x[1 + (1/x) - (1/(2x²))] = x + 1 - (1/(2x))

Subtracting x from both sides gives:

x√(1 + (2/x)) - x ≈ 1 - (1/(2x))

As x approaches infinity, the term 1/(2x) becomes negligible, so the limit is 1.

To find the limit of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \) as \( x \) approaches infinity, we can use a method that involves rationalizing the expression. Here's a step-by-step solution:

**Given:**
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right)
\]

**Step 1: Rationalize the Expression**

To simplify the expression, multiply and divide by the conjugate of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \), which is \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x \):

\[
\left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right) \times \frac{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

**Step 2: Multiply Out the Numerator**

\[
= \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x})^2 - x^2}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]
\[
= \frac{x^2 + 2x - x^2}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]
\[
= \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

**Step 3: Simplify the Denominator**

Factor out \( x \) from the square root in the denominator:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = x \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
\]

So, the expression becomes:

\[
= \frac{2x}{x \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + x}
\]
\[
= \frac{2x}{x \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1 \right)}
\]

**Step 4: Cancel Out \( x \) in the Numerator and Denominator**

\[
= \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
\]

**Step 5: Take the Limit as \( x \) Approaches Infinity**

As \( x \to \infty \), \( \frac{2}{x} \to 0 \). Therefore:

\[
= \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 0} + 1}
\]
\[
= \frac{2}{1 + 1}
\]
\[
= \frac{2}{2}
\]
\[
= 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{1}
\]

The limit as \( x \) approaches \( 0^+ \) of \( \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} \) can be found using L’Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach zero, creating a \( \frac{0}{0} \) indeterminate form. Applying the rule involves taking derivatives:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}\ln(1+x)}{\frac{d}{dx}x} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = 1
\]

   - lim_{x→∞} ln(y) = 1.
   - Therefore, y → e^1 = e.

To find the derivative of the polynomial \( x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \) with respect to \( x \), we'll apply the **power rule** to each term. The power rule states that if you have a function like \( x^n \), its derivative is \( n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

1. **Derivative of \( x^3 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^{2}
   \]

2. **Derivative of \( 2x^2 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(2x^2) = 4x
   \]
   (We multiplied the coefficient 2 by the exponent 2 and then reduced the exponent by 1.)

3. **Derivative of \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
   \]
   (The derivative of \( x^1 \) is \( 1 \cdot x^{0} = 1 \).)

4. **Derivative of the constant term \( 1 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0
   \]

Now, combine all these derivatives:

\[
3x^{2} + 4x + 1 + 0 = 3x^{2} + 4x + 1
\]

\boxed{3x^{2} + 4x + 1}

The derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is found using the product rule:

\[
F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Functions:** Let \( u(x) = \cos x \) and \( v(x) = \ln x \).
2. **Differentiate Each Function:**
   - The derivative of \( u(x) = \cos x \) is \( u'(x) = -\sin x \).
   - The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) is \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).
3. **Apply the Product Rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
   \]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we can use the chain rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Rewrite the Function:**
   \[
   F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   - Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \), so \( F(u) = u^{-1} \).
   - The derivative of \( F \) with respect to \( u \) is:
     \[
     \frac{dF}{du} = -u^{-2}
     \]
   - The derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
     \]

3. **Combine the Derivatives:**
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{dF}{du} \times \frac{du}{dx} = (-u^{-2}) \times (2x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

To find the derivative of \( E^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the **chain rule**. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Outer function (\( f(g) \)): \( E^{g} \)
   - Inner function (\( g(x) \)): \( x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to the inner function:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dg} E^{g} = E^{g}
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x
   \]

4. **Apply the chain rule by multiplying the derivatives:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} E^{x^2} = E^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2xE^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\dfrac{2 x E^{x^{2}}}{1}}

- Numerator (\( u \)) = \( \ln x \)
- Denominator (\( v \)) = \( x^2 \)

**Step 1:** Let F(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x). The numerator g(x) is e^x, and the denominator f(x) is 1 + e^x.

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) is calculated as follows:

Given \( y = \tan^{-1} x \), we have:
\[
\tan(y) = x
\]

Differentiating both sides with respect to \(x\):
\[
\sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Solving for \(\frac{dy}{dx}\):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
\]

Using the identity \( \tan^2(y) + 1 = \sec^2(y) \), and substituting \( \tan(y) = x \):
\[
\sec^2(y) = 1 + x^2
\]

Therefore:
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
\]

**Answer:**
The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) is \(\frac{1}{1+x^2}\).

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the quotient rule.

**Step 1: Identify f(x) and g(x)**  
Let \( f(x) = x \) and \( g(x) = 1 + x^2 \).

**Step 2: Find f'(x) and g'(x)**  
\( f'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of x is 1)  
\( g'(x) = 2x \) (using the power rule on \( x^2 \))

**Step 3: Apply the quotient rule formula**
\[
F'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
\]
Substituting the known values:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the numerator**
\[
(1)(1 + x^2) = 1 + x^2
\]
\[
(x)(2x) = 2x^2
\]
Subtracting:
\[
1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

**Step 5: Write the final derivative**
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

To find the derivative of \( \sec(x) \), we start by expressing \( \sec(x) \) as \( \frac{1}{\cos(x)} \). Applying the quotient rule:

Let \( g(x) = 1 \) and \( h(x) = \cos(x) \).

- \( g'(x) = 0 \)
- \( h'(x) = -\sin(x) \)

Using the quotient rule formula:
\[ f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} \]
\[ f'(x) = \frac{0 \cdot \cos(x) - 1 \cdot (-\sin(x))}{\cos^2(x)} \]
\[ f'(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)} \]

Recognizing that \( \sec(x) = \frac{1}{\cos(x)} \) and \( \tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)} \), we can rewrite the derivative as:
\[ f'(x) = \sec(x)\tan(x) \]

**Final Answer**
{'Final Answer' : 'sec(x)*tan(x)'}
